### Pass 271 — the North America cell refuted itself by arithmetic, and the regional figures are ONE vendor's page

⏱️ **Seventeenth pass of `2026-10-17`.** Evidence:
[`p271-websearch.2026-10-17-p271.tsv`](../evidence/p271-websearch.2026-10-17-p271.tsv) *(14 legs; legs
1–4 and 5–6 were taken verbatim from `p270`'s own recommendation and its unrun costed step)* ·
[`verified-repos.2026-10-17-p271.tsv`](../evidence/verified-repos.2026-10-17-p271.tsv).

`p270` closed by naming the next targets: *"the NA `$3.68B` and MEA `$0.56B` figures are now the obvious
next re-verification targets, since each rests on **one** publisher and neither has ever been tested."*
**Both were tested. Both moved.**

#### 🔴 The NA cell was internally impossible, and no search was needed to see it

This base carried **$3.68B (2026) → $32B (2030) @ 31.1% CAGR**. Those three numbers cannot all be true:

```
3.68 × (1.311)^4  =  10.87      ← what the carried CAGR actually produces
$32B from $3.68B in 4 years     →  requires a 71.7% CAGR, not 31.1%
```

🟢 **And the publisher, unrecorded for ~200 passes, is now named: ResourcERA — which publishes
`$3.68B (2026) → $10.8B by 2030 @ 31.1%`.** The arithmetic and the publisher agree with each other and
both disagree with this base. **The horizon is corrected to `$10.8B`.**

🔴 **The mechanism is worth more than the correction, because it is reproducible.** Look at what this
file already carried two tables apart:

| | 2026 baseline | 2030 horizon | CAGR |
|---|---|---|---|
| ResourcERA, **global** | $10.4B | **$32.27B** | 31.2% |
| this base, **North America** | $3.68B | **$32B** | 31.1% |

🔵 **The regional row kept ResourcERA's regional baseline and regional CAGR and took ResourcERA's
GLOBAL horizon.** The two CAGRs differ by one tenth of a point, which is exactly why nobody caught it:
the row looked internally consistent to a reader checking the *source*, and was wrong to a reader
checking the *multiplication*. A client briefed from this cell would have been told the North American
market reaches **three times** the size its own stated growth rate produces.

🟢 **New check, cheap and total — `T32`: for every market cell, verify `baseline × (1+CAGR)^years ≈
horizon` before publishing it.** Run against all four regional rows:

| row | baseline × (1+CAGR)^n | carried horizon | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| EMEA $2.64B @ 31.9% | **7.99** | $8.0B | 🟢 consistent |
| LATAM $0.67B @ 33.5% | **2.13** | $2.1B | 🟢 consistent |
| NA (ResourcERA) $3.68B @ 31.1% | **10.87** | $10.8B | 🟢 consistent |
| **NA (as this base carried it)** | **10.87** | **$32B** | 🔴 **2.9× mismatch** |

🔵 **This is a new defect class for this base.** The five defects `p266`–`p270` found were all
*cross-shelf* — a cell disagreeing with another file, found by grepping. **This one is intra-cell**: the
row disagrees with *itself*, and no amount of grepping finds it. **Arithmetic is a second oracle, and
this base has not been using it.**

#### 🔴 The regional figures are not four independent sources. They are one vendor's page

ResourcERA's signature is unmistakable once named: **2026 baseline, 2030 horizon, CAGR in the 31–34%
band.** Every regional row this base carries fits it — NA `$3.68B @ 31.1%`, EMEA `$2.64B @ 31.9%`,
LATAM `$0.67B @ 33.5%`, MEA `$0.56B @ 34.3%`. 🟡 **Stated as a strong inference, not as established**:
`resourcera.com` cannot be opened from this environment (see the channel note), so the attribution rests
on the two rows whose numbers search returned verbatim (NA and LATAM) plus the shared signature.

🟢 **It is testable anyway, and it passes.** If those four are one publisher's decomposition, they
should sum toward that publisher's own global baseline:

```
NA 3.68 + EMEA 2.64 + MEA 0.56 + LATAM 0.67  =  7.55
ResourcERA global baseline                   =  10.40
implied APAC residual                        =   2.85
```

🟢 **And the residual is independently corroborated.** Grand View puts APAC at **~$2.28B (2025) @ 28.1%**
→ **~$2.92B for 2026** — **within 2.5% of the $2.85B residual**, by a different publisher using a
different method. **This base can now publish an APAC market figure for the first time**, flagged as
*derived*. 🔵 It also means `T30`'s warning was understated: the regional picture is not "one publisher
per region", it is **one publisher for the whole regional picture**.

#### 🔴 And the same aggregator sentence is the sole source of a figure this base quotes and a claim it retracted

`p270` retracted *"Finland / Estonia / Netherlands as K-12 leaders"* because it *"traces ONLY to an
aggregator citing no source."* Pass 271's EMEA leg returned that aggregator, and here is the whole of it:

> the European AI in education market at **$2.64 billion in 2026**, forecast to grow at **31.9% CAGR** to
> **$8.0 billion by 2030**, with **Finland, Estonia, and the Netherlands leading in K-12 AI integration**

🔴 **One sentence. This base retracted its second half as unsourced and kept its first half as a market
figure** — and `p270` even re-published that figure as one half of its new two-publisher range. 🔵 **A
source is not reliable per-clause.** 🟢 The honest posture, now adopted: the `$2.64B` end of the EMEA
range carries **the same grade as the claim this base threw away**, and MarketDataForecast's `~$2.11B` is
the end of that range with an identifiable publisher behind it. New trend **`T33`**.

#### 🟢 MEA: this file declared the region UNSIZED while the base held a figure for it

`p270` wrote, in this file's declared gaps: *"MEA remains UNSIZED, unchanged from `p269`. This pass added
no MEA figure."* 🔴 **`grep` says otherwise** — `agents/trending.md:18650` has carried
**MEA $0.56B (2026), 34.3% CAGR** since an earlier pass, and `intel/open-gaps.md` and `intel/trends.md`
both cite it by value.

🔵 **This is the Sanoma defect inverted, and the inversion is the interesting part.** `p270`'s defect was
**a false claim of PRESENCE** (*"recorded in market.md"* — it was not). This is **a false claim of
ABSENCE** (*"MEA remains UNSIZED"* — it was sized, elsewhere). 🟢 Both are statements *about* the shelves
that disagree with the shelves; `T29`'s amendment catches the first shape and **misses the second**,
because a claim of absence is confirmed by the very silence it describes. **Amendment (`T34`): a declared
gap must be greped across the OTHER shelves before it is re-declared.** This is the **sixth cross-shelf
defect in six passes.**

🟢 **MEA is now sized, with named publishers and first-hand grading:**

| Publisher | Scope | Baseline | Horizon | CAGR |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Grand View Research** | MEA, AI in education | **$280.8M (2024)** | **$1,645.9M (2030)** | **32.6%** |
| Grand View Research | MEA, **AI tutors** sub-segment | $188.5M (2025) | $1,736.8M (2033) | 32% |
| Transpire Insight | MEA, **AI tutors** | $186.4M (2025) | $1,730.02M (2033) | 32.13% |
| ~~Cognitive Market Research~~ | MEA, AI-in-education tech | — | — | ~~47.2%~~ |

⚠️ **The two AI-tutors rows agree to within 1.1% on the baseline and 0.4% on the horizon. Treat them as
ONE source, not two** — agreement that tight across independent houses is a derivation, not a
corroboration. 🔵 **This base now has a rule for it and it is the opposite of the usual one: suspiciously
close agreement is weaker evidence than a visible disagreement.**

🔴 **Cognitive Market Research's 47.2% is REFUSED, and the reason belongs in this file.** Its headline
dates conflict (2023 base, a 2024–2030 window, a 2025–2033 CAGR label) and **its country table is
largely placeholders.** 🔵 That is this brief's own *"never leave a header or example row as data"*
hazard — found in a commercial research product being sold as a regional market report.

🟡 **Reconciliation with the carried figure:** Grand View's trajectory gives **~$494M for 2026** against
the carried **$560M** — ~13% apart, *closer* than EMEA's 25%, and the CAGRs (32.6% vs 34.3%) are 1.7
points apart. Carried value retained as the ResourcERA end of a range; **not merged.**

⚠️ **And Grand View's own MEA row does not quite reconcile with itself** — flagged here because
`T32` was written this pass and must be applied to this pass's own additions first. **$280.8M (2024) →
$1,645.9M (2030)** closes **only** if the **32.6%** CAGR is applied to its *labelled* **2025–2030**
window, which requires a **+43% single year** from 2024 to 2025. 🔵 **Almost certainly two report
vintages rather than one growth curve** — so quote the **2030 horizon with the CAGR**, or the **2024
base alone**, but do not present them as one series. 🟢 **The AI-tutors rows, by contrast, reconcile to
0.1%** — which is also why they should be read as one derived model rather than two publishers
(above).

#### 🔵 NA has three publishers and they span 3.9× on the same region

| Publisher | Baseline | Horizon | CAGR | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **ResourcERA** | **$3.68B (2026)** | $10.8B (2030) | 31.1% | the figure this base carries |
| Grand View Research | $3,113.8M (2025) | ~$19.4B (2033) | 24.2% | the conservative read |
| MarketsandMarkets | $951M (2024) | $2.3B (2029) | 15.9% | **3.9× below GVR for the same region.** 🔴 **`p274`: this row is ALSO one-year-mislabelled — it closes to +0.22% across SIX years, not the labelled five, and it was never in `p272`'s 19-cell sweep.** So the window defect `T38` attributes to Grand View is **not a Grand View quirk** |

🔵 **The spread is definitional, not factual** — US-only vs. all of North America, software-only vs.

### Pass 272 — the regional picture has ONE upstream publisher, and this base was checking Grand View against Grand View

⏱️ **Eighteenth pass of `2026-10-17`.** Evidence:
[`p272-websearch.2026-10-17-p272.tsv`](../evidence/p272-websearch.2026-10-17-p272.tsv) *(9 legs; legs
1–5 taken verbatim from `p271`'s ranked backlog)* ·
[`arithmetic-sweep.2026-10-17-p272.tsv`](../evidence/arithmetic-sweep.2026-10-17-p272.tsv) *(19 cells,
zero searches)* ·
[`verified-repos.2026-10-17-p272.tsv`](../evidence/verified-repos.2026-10-17-p272.tsv) *(5 repositories)*.

`p271` closed by naming its own weakest claim and asking for it to be attacked: *"Falsify the ResourcERA
attribution — the one claim this pass made on inference rather than reading … Cheapest falsification: one
leg per region quoting ResourcERA by name."* **Those legs were run. The attribution was half wrong, and
what replaced it is worse than what `p271` feared.**

#### 🔴 1. The North America publisher is Technotrenz, not ResourcERA

`p271` recorded *"Publisher identified for the first time in ~200 passes — **ResourcERA**"*. Asked
directly, the leg says the `$3.68B → $10.80B @ 31.1%` row **"is not a ResourcERA figure"**; it is
**Technotrenz**. ResourcERA publishes NA as a **36% share** of its 2026 base, and `0.36 × $10.4B = $3.744B`
— **within 1.7% of Technotrenz's $3.68B**.

🔵 **So the mechanism is not "one vendor's decomposition". It is a *percentage* that a second aggregator
turned into *dollars*.** A share is a claim about proportion; a dollar figure is a claim about magnitude.
The conversion manufactures the second one and it arrives looking like independent regional research.
**Technotrenz had never been named anywhere on this base** — not on a shelf, not in the register.

#### 🔴 2. ResourcERA is not the upstream. Grand View Research is — and the proof is arithmetic, not a citation

Three independent fingerprints, none of which needed the page to be openable:

| fingerprint | test | result |
|---|---|---|
| **Global** | ResourcERA's `$32.27B by 2030 @ 31.2%` | 🔴 **Verbatim Grand View.** GVR's own press release: *"expected to reach USD 32.27 billion by 2030, registering a CAGR of 31.2% from 2025 to 2030"* |
| **Europe** | GVR's data-tree base `$1,520.1M` (**year not stated on the page**) compounded at GVR's own 31.9% | 🔴 Arithmetic fixes the base year at **2024** (`1520.1 × 1.319⁶ = 8004.6` vs GVR's published `8003.4`, **+0.02%**). Compound it **two** years instead: `1520.1 × 1.319² = $2,644.6M` — **ResourcERA's EMEA figure is $2.64B, a 0.17% match** |
| **MEA** | the CAGR *implied* by GVR's MEA endpoints (`$280.8M → $1,645.9M`, 6y) | 🔴 **34.3% — exactly ResourcERA's labelled MEA CAGR** |

🔴 **Consequence for `p271`'s headline result.** `p271` derived APAC as the residual of the regional sum
and wrote: *"the implied APAC residual lands within **2.5%** of Grand View's independent **~$2.92B**"*,
calling it *"corroborated … by a different method"*. **Grand View is not independent of ResourcERA; it is
ResourcERA's source.** The residual was checked against the very model it was extracted from. 🟢 **A 2.5%
agreement produced that way is guaranteed, not evidential** — and the tighter it is, the more convincing
the circularity looks. `T36`.

#### 🔴 3. The APAC cell was derived while a published figure sat in this same file

This file's headline table published `~$2.85B (2026) — derived, not published`. **Line ~147 of the same
file already carried Grand View's directly published `$2,282.9M (2025) @ 28.1% → ~$18.2B (2033)`** — used
as *corroboration* of the derived number rather than as a **replacement** for it.

🔵 **A new defect species: INTRA-FILE, headline-versus-body.** `p266`–`p270` found cross-shelf defects
(`grep` for disagreement between files); `p271` found the first intra-cell one (a row refuting itself).
**This one is a file disagreeing with itself across 120 lines, where the better datum is the one further
down.** No cross-shelf grep reaches it — both readings live in the same file — and no arithmetic check
reaches it either, because **both cells are internally sound.**

#### 🔴 4. Grand View mislabels its own CAGR window — in three regional databooks — and this base had filed the symptom as rounding noise

`p271`'s costed item 2 (*"run `T32`'s arithmetic check across EVERY remaining numeric cell … it needs zero
searches"*) was run: **19 cells, full ledger in
[`arithmetic-sweep.2026-10-17-p272.tsv`](../evidence/arithmetic-sweep.2026-10-17-p272.tsv).** 🟢 **No cell
on these shelves is impossible any more** — `p271`'s NA defect was the only `STRUCTURAL` one and it is
fixed. **Two cells `FLAG`, and they are the same defect:**

| cell | as the publisher LABELS the window | one year earlier |
|---|---|---|
| **EMEA** GVR `$1,520.1M → $8,003.4M @ 31.9%` | labelled **2025–2030** → **+31.9%** 🔴 `FLAG` | **2024–2030** → **−0.0%** 🟢 |
| **MEA** GVR `$280.8M → $1,645.9M @ 32.6%` | labelled **2025–2030** → **+43.0%** 🔴 `FLAG` | **2024–2030** → **+7.8%** 🟢 |
| **APAC** GVR `$2,282.9M → $18,214.1M @ 28.1%` | baseline **2025** → **+10.0%** | window **2026–2033** → **+0.0%** 🟢 |

🔵 **The APAC row is the one that matters, because this base had already measured the symptom and
dismissed it.** `intel/trends.md` carries that row with a `10%` mismatch annotated **"base stated as
'roughly'"** — i.e. filed as rounding. 🟢 **It is not rounding. It is a one-year window offset, and it
resolves to +0.0%.** Reading the baseline year off the stated CAGR window — rather than off the number
beside it — moves APAC's 2026 point from **$2.92B to $3.22B**, a 10% revision to a regional figure a
studio would size an engagement from.

⚠️ **Standing instruction, cheap and repeatable: a Grand View horizon page's CAGR window and its printed
baseline year can disagree by a year. Derive the baseline from the horizon and the CAGR; do not trust the
label.** And `T32` now has a second duty: **a 10% mismatch is not automatically noise — test the
window before writing it off.**

#### 🟢 What a studio should actually quote after this pass

| For | Quote | Why |
|---|---|---|
| **Global** | **$11.4B (2026) → $57.2B (2033), 25.9%** — Grand View, current vintage | The research house's own number, current release, closes to −0.0% |
| **North America** | **$4.25B (2026) → $19.39B (2033), 24.2%** — Grand View | Publisher's own; replaces an aggregator's conversion of a share |
| **APAC** | **$3.22B (2026) → $18.21B (2033), 28.1%** — Grand View | Published, not derived; window-corrected |
| **EMEA** | **~$2.11B (2026)** — MarketDataForecast | The one EMEA figure that is **not** Grand View's curve re-based |
| **LATAM** | 🔴 **a range, never a point — and `p274` corrects its ENDS: $132M–$670M for 2026**, not `$105.6M–$670M` | 🔴 **`$105.6M` is MarketsandMarkets' *2024* print.** Carried to 2026 on M&M's own 11.7% it is **$132M**, so the published range compared a 2024 endpoint against two 2026 endpoints. **The 2026-consistent spread is 5.1×, not ~6×** — and here the spread *is* the finding, so its size matters. Grand View's own 2026 LATAM point is itself **two numbers** (**$455M** forward from its 2024 base, **$490M** back from its 2030 horizon, 7.1% apart) because that row is a splice. Still the only region where ResourcERA does not reconcile to Grand View |
| **MEA** | **$280.8M (2024) → $1,645.9M (2030), 32.6%** — Grand View | Unchanged from `p271`; window caveat above |

⚠️ **And the honest framing for a client deck:** the regional AI-in-education picture rests, as far as this
base can establish, on **one research house's model**, re-based and re-published by at least three
aggregators. **Independent corroboration of these regional numbers has not been found. It has only ever
been simulated by re-publication.**

### Pass 273 — the independent-publisher count goes UP, and the two most independent houses disagree 1.48x at the SAME year

`p272` ended on a bleak structural finding: the regional picture had **one upstream publisher**, and this
base had been checking Grand View against Grand View. 🟢 **Pass 273 improves that, which is worth stating
plainly because this register usually moves the other way.**

🔴 **First, the defect, and it is `p272`'s species one row down.** The forecast-spread table credited its
largest-CAGR row to **"Research and Markets."** ResearchAndMarkets.com is a **report storefront** — a
distributor. 🟢 **The research house is THE BUSINESS RESEARCH COMPANY**: *AI in Education Market Report
2026*, published **February 2026**, 250 pages, with the forecast carried in a ResearchAndMarkets press
release on GlobeNewswire dated **2026-04-07**. 🔵 **`p272` caught an aggregator wearing a publisher's name
(ResourcERA → Grand View). This is the same error in a different costume — a *distributor* wearing one —
and it had been sitting in the table the whole time.**

🟢 **The correction is good news, and that is the unusual part.** TBRC is **not** a Grand View
re-publisher: it runs its own model, on its own vintage, to its own horizon. So the honest count of
**independent models** behind this shelf is **at least three** — TBRC, Grand View, and the
Precedence/MarkWide pair — not the one `p272` feared.

🔵 **And now that two independent houses can be compared at a COMMON year, the finding is sharper than
any spread this file has published.** The horizons differ (2030 vs 2033), so neither number can be quoted
against the other as published. Carried to the same year on each publisher's own CAGR:

| | TBRC | Grand View (current vintage) |
|---|---|---|
| 2026 baseline | $10.6B | $11.4B — **TBRC is 7% LOWER today** |
| 2030 | **$42.48B** (published) | **$28.64B** (derived from its own 25.9%) |
| ratio at 2030 | — | 🔴 **1.48x apart** |

🔴 **The two houses that agree most closely about today's market disagree by half again about the one
four years out, and they diverge in the opposite direction from their baselines.** 🔵 **That is the
sentence to put in a deck** — not a market size. A TAM slide sourced to TBRC and the same slide sourced to
Grand View differ by **$13.8B at 2030**, and both are defensible.

🟢 **The `T32` arithmetic sweep ran over all four independent rows and came back CLEAN** — the first
zero-`FLAG` sweep recorded on this shelf. Each publisher's own CAGR carries its own baseline to its own
published horizon: TBRC `+0.03%`, Grand View `−0.07%`, MarkWide `+0.00%`, Precedence `+1.02%` (the
largest residual here, and still a pass). 🔵 **So the disagreement between these houses is a disagreement
about the world, not an arithmetic error in anybody's spreadsheet** — which is precisely why it cannot be
resolved by picking the one that looks most careful.

⚠️ **Channel, restated rather than inherited:** `precedenceresearch.com` and `finance.yahoo.com` are both
**ENOTFOUND** on the fetch channel this pass, as `grandviewresearch.com` has been for six passes. **Every
figure above is secondary-source grade, read through the search tool only.**

⏱️ **Twentieth pass of `2026-10-17`.** Evidence:
[`arithmetic-sweep.2026-10-17-p274.tsv`](../evidence/arithmetic-sweep.2026-10-17-p274.tsv) *(28 cells,
zero searches)* · [`p274-websearch.2026-10-17-p274.tsv`](../evidence/p274-websearch.2026-10-17-p274.tsv)

`p273` closed by naming its highest-value step: *"Read `market.md` top-to-bottom as a reader, and diff
its headline table against its own body … Zero searches. Highest expected yield on this base."*
**It was read, end to end, and the yield is in the headline table.**

#### 🔴 1. `p272`'s replacement cell inherited the defect of the cell it replaced

`p271` found the North America row impossible because it **kept one vintage's baseline and took another
vintage's horizon**. `p272` then wrote the global row this file has told every reader to quote since:

> **$8.3B (2025) / $11.4B (2026) → $57.2B (2033), 25.9% CAGR** — *"Closes to −0.0% under `T32`"*

🔴 **Only the second half closes.** Three lines of arithmetic, no search:

```
11.4 × 1.259^7  =  57.16   vs  57.2   →  −0.07 %   ← the current vintage, sound
 8.3 × 1.259^8  =  52.39   vs  57.2   →  −8.40 %   ← the printed 2025 figure cannot reach it
 8.3 → 11.4 in one year    =  +37.3 %  against a labelled 25.9 %
 8.3 × 1.312^5  =  32.27   vs  32.27  →  −0.01 %   ← what $8.3B actually belongs to
```

🔵 **The `$8.3B` is the SUPERSEDED release's baseline**, and this file carries the proof two tables
down: `$8.3B (2025) → $32.27B (2030) @ 31.2%` closes to a hundredth of a percent. 🟢 **So the cell was
a two-vintage splice, and `T32`'s own verdict was quoted over it** — the check ran on one leg of a
two-leg cell and the pass reported the result as if it covered both. **The `$8.3B` is removed from the
headline row.**

#### 🔴 2. The ~−9 % Grand View signature is a THIRD instance, and it is in the global cell

`T38` (`p272`) established that on Grand View's 2033-vintage databooks the printed baseline year
disagrees with the labelled CAGR window, and tabled **four** regional cells. 🔴 **The global cell has
the same signature and was not in the table:**

| Grand View, 2033 vintage | printed base vs its own curve |
|---|---|
| **Global** `$8.3B → $57.2B @ 25.9%` | 🔴 **−8.40 %** — *new in `p274`, and it is the headline* |
| **North America** `$3,113.8M → $19,388.3M @ 24.2%` | 🔴 **−9.07 %** |
| **APAC** `$2,282.9M → $18,214.1M @ 28.1%` | 🔴 **−9.12 %** |

🟢 **Three databooks from one house, clustering inside a 0.7-point band. That is a signature, not
noise** — and it converts `T38`'s standing rule from a regional caution into a house-wide one:
**derive the baseline from the horizon and the CAGR on every Grand View 2033-vintage figure, the
global one included.**

#### 🔴 3. And the defect is not Grand View's. MarketsandMarkets does it too — in two cells this shelf quotes

🔵 **`p272`'s 19-cell ledger marked four mismatches `ok` without running the window test its own covering
note had just mandated** (*"a 10% mismatch is not automatically noise — test the window before writing it
off"*). `p274` ran that test on every cell, **including the two M&M rows the sweep never contained:**

| cell | as LABELLED | read one year LONGER | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| **NA, M&M** `$951M (2024) → $2.3B (2029) @ 15.9%` | **−13.53 %** | **+0.22 %** 🟢 | 🔴 **WINDOW_FIX — never swept** |
| **LATAM, M&M** `$105.6M (2024) → $204.7M (2029) @ 11.7%` | **−10.30 %** | **+0.20 %** 🟢 | 🔴 **WINDOW_FIX — swept, and marked `ok`** |

🟢 **Two houses, five cells, one convention.** `T38` is titled *"a publisher can mislabel its own CAGR
window"* and reads, in practice, as a caution about Grand View. **It is a property of this literature**:
a printed "current year" figure and a labelled forecast window are produced by different parts of a
report and are routinely a year apart. ⚠️ **The operational consequence is a rule change, not a new
caveat:** a window test costs one multiplication, so **run it on every cell and retire the threshold.**
`p271`'s *">25 % flag, >50 % investigate"* would not have fired on **any** of the five.

#### 🔴 4. Nine of the 28 cells are SPLICES — they close at no window — and three of them are on this shelf as series

A cell that closes at neither the labelled span nor ±1 year is **two report vintages, not one growth
curve**. This file already says so for Grand View's MEA row. 🔴 **It does not say so for the LATAM row,
which is where the `$455M` 2026 point a studio would quote comes from:**

| cell | mismatch as labelled | closes at ±1 y? | what this shelf says |
|---|---|---|---|
| **MEA GVR** `$280.8M → $1,645.9M @ 32.6%` | −7.26 % | 🔴 no | 🟢 *"almost certainly two report vintages"* — correct |
| **LATAM GVR** `$259.6M → $1,505.6M @ 32.4%` | −7.12 % | 🔴 no | 🔴 **`T32` filed it as *"window rounding"* 🟢. It is not.** Its 2026 point is **$455M** forward or **$490M** back — **7.1 % apart, and only one of them is published anywhere** |
| **MEA ResourcERA** `$0.56B → $1.6B @ 34.3%` | +13.86 % | 🔴 no | 🔴 marked `ok` by `p272` at −12.2 % under the opposite sign convention |

⚠️ **Sign conventions differ between the two ledgers and that is itself part of the problem.** `p272`
states mismatch as `published / projected − 1`; `p274` states it as `projected / published − 1`. **The
same cell reads `+11.5 %` there and `−10.3 %` here.** A single "10 % threshold" was never comparable
across the two.

#### 🟢 5. `p273`'s backlog item 2 is DISCHARGED: this shelf now has one regional figure that is provably not Grand View re-based

`p273` asked for *"ONE regional figure from a publisher that is provably not Grand View"*, and named the

**MarketDataForecast's Europe figure passes it.**

| test | result |
|---|---|
| Does MDF re-base Grand View? | 🟢 **No.** GVR's Europe curve puts 2025 at `1,520.1 × 1.319 = $2,005M`. **MDF prints $1,590M — 21 % below it.** No compounding relation at any plausible window produces one from the other |
| Is MDF internally sound? | 🟢 **Yes, and it is the ONLY regional cell on this shelf that closes AS LABELLED.** `$1.59B (2025) → ~$20B (2034) @ 32.47%` → **−0.13 %**; its own 2026 point `$2.11B` → **−0.18 %**. No window fix needed |
| Independent *model*, or independent *name*? | 🟡 **The honest answer, stated rather than implied.** The reproduction test clears it. A second test does not settle it: MDF's `$1,590M (2025)` sits only **4.6 % above** GVR's `$1,520.1M (2024)` — a near-identical magnitude a year apart — and their CAGRs differ by **0.57 pt**. What separates them is the **horizon** (2034 vs 2030) and the failed re-basing. 🔵 **Claim exactly this much: MDF is not a re-publication of Grand View's curve. Whether it is an independent model is not established** |

🟢 **So the EMEA number to quote is MarketDataForecast's `~$2.11B (2026)`** — unchanged as a

### Pass 275 — an independent search path re-derived Grand View's current vintage, and the arithmetic says which PAIR a studio may quote

🟢 **`p272` established that the `$32.27B by 2030` line every aggregator copies is a *superseded*
Grand View release, and that the current one is `$8.3B (2025) / $11.4B (2026) → $57.2B (2033) @ 25.9%`.
`p275`'s trends leg reached the same current vintage by a different route** — an aggregator with no
visible relation to the path `p272` used reports *"USD 8.3 billion in 2025, rising to USD 57.2 billion
by 2033."* 🔵 This is **corroboration of a vintage, not of a value**: the same house still stands behind
it, so it does not make the number independent. What it does establish is that the current release is
the one in circulation, and this base is no longer quoting a release the publisher has moved off.

🔴 **And the triple does not close as stated — `T38`, measured again on a cell this base publishes.**

| window | endpoints | implied CAGR | stated |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2025 → 2033 (8 y) | `$8.3B → $57.2B` | 🔴 **27.29%** | 25.9% |
| **2026 → 2033 (7 y)** | **`$11.4B → $57.2B`** | 🟢 **25.91%** | **25.9%** ✓ |

🟢 **So the `25.9%` belongs to the 2026 base, not the 2025 one** — it closes to within `0.01` point on
the 2026→2033 window and misses by `1.4` points on 2025→2033. This is the **same one-year labelling
offset** `p274` found in four Grand View regional databooks (`T38`), now confirmed on the **global**
headline from an independently-reached copy. **It is a publisher-side habit, not a transcription slip.**

⚠️ **Operational rule for a studio deck:** quote **`$11.4B (2026) → $57.2B (2033) @ 25.9%`** as one
coherent triple. Quoting *"$8.3B in 2025, growing 25.9%"* splices two windows and overstates the 2025
base's growth — and it is the splice `p274` caught this base making once already.

#### Two further publishers, which widen the spread rather than settle it

| publisher | base | horizon | implied CAGR | note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **IMARC** | `$6.4B` (2025) | `$79.6B` (2034) | **32.3%** *(derived; none stated)* | The **most aggressive** forecast on this shelf: a higher 2034 endpoint than Grand View's 2033 off a **lower** base |
| **The Business Research Company** | `$7.52B` (2025) | `$10.6B` (**2026**) | **41.0% in one year** | 🔴 **Read the horizon.** This is a *one-year* pair, not a forecast to 2030+ |

🔴 **`$10.6B` is the number this base's own cycle-2 notes once published as the education-AI market
size, and it is TBRC's 2026 point** — a 2026 *endpoint* from the house with the highest near-term growth
rate, carried as though it were the consensus size. 🔵 **Three houses now put 2025 between `$6.4B` and
`$8.3B` — a 1.30x spread at the same year, on the same definition.** The spread is still the finding.

## Global players

| Player | Type | AI position | Opening for Globant |
|--------|------|-------------|---------------------|
| **Instructure (Canvas)** | Commercial SaaS | Native AI grading and content assistance | Migration path to Open edX/Moodle with an equivalent AI layer the client owns |
| **Blackboard / Anthology** | Commercial SaaS | Scale in North American higher ed | Replacement engagements; the incumbent's lock-in is the pitch |
| **Khanmigo (Khan Academy)** | Nonprofit | Socratic tutoring at national scale | UX reference, not a technical base — it is not open source |
| **Duolingo** | Consumer | Roleplay and voice tutoring | The consumer bar for engagement that institutional work gets compared against |
| **Moodle HQ** | Open source steward | First-party AI subsystem since 4.5, provider-pluggable | Partner, not competitor: the subsystem's gaps (question generation, conversational practice, SCORM) are studio work |
| **Microsoft Education** | Cloud/Enterprise | Agentic admin workflows + tutoring, Azure-coupled | Globant as the cloud-neutral implementer |
| **TUM / `ls1intum`** | Academic | Artemis: automated feedback at university scale, MIT | Adopt and extend; the licence invites it |
| **Selleo** | Vendor + OSS | Mentingo, MIT AI-native LMS | Build-on or partner for corporate L&D |
| **Teachy** (BR) | Commercial, LATAM | AI lesson preparation for teachers; has expanded out of the region into Asia | The regional incumbent in exactly the teacher-facing niche this KB says is the funded one. Partner or benchmark, not a base. |
| **Ginia** (MX) | Commercial, LATAM | AI-driven employability | Adjacent: the skills-to-jobs end of the funnel a tutoring engagement stops short of. |
| **Vetto** (BR) | Commercial, LATAM | Listed in HolonIQ's 2026 LATAM EdTech 100 | Regional landscape awareness. |
| **Portabilis / Comunidade i-Educar** (BR) | Open source steward | `i-educar` (GPL-2.0) + `i-diario` (AGPL-3.0), both committed to in October 2026 | **The LATAM open-source partner this KB had not found.** Federal software-public provenance; an agentic layer over the installed base is the engagement. |
| **OpenAI × California State University** | Commercial deployment, NA | Reported as the **largest organization-wide ChatGPT deployment to date**, across the CSU system | 🟢 **`p274`.** The higher-ed counterpart to Utah/Gemini in K-12. **The engagement sits BESIDE it** — integration surface, governance artefact, curriculum-bound work the general assistant cannot do. Not a platform contest |
| **Anthropic × Northeastern** | Commercial deployment, NA | **Claude for Higher Education** | 🟢 **`p274`.** Second named higher-ed single-vendor procurement. Same posture as above: be the layer the assistant cannot be |
| **Platzi** (CO) | Commercial, LATAM | Named with Teachy among the region's mature AI-positioned platforms | 🟢 **`p274`, new to this base.** Regional landscape awareness; consumer/professional skilling rather than institutional |
| **UPlanner** (CL) | Commercial, LATAM | Institutional data for **dropout prevention** | 🟢 **`p274`, new to this base — and it occupies the exact lane this file calls the open one.** Dropout prediction is institutional plumbing, which is what Teachy does *not* sell. **Benchmark or partner; do not pitch it as greenfield** |
| **SEA+** (UY) | Platform, LATAM | **Adaptive assessment** | 🟢 **`p274`, new to this base.** ⚠️ A platform **name**, not a repository; Ceibal is established here as publishing no public code. **Integration, not adoption** |
| **UNESCO / IDB** (LAC) | Multilateral | UNESCO's regional **AI-in-education Observatory for LAC**; IDB's review of **193 regional AI initiatives** | Funders and standard-setters. Being referenced in their frameworks is worth more than any single pilot. |

**What HolonIQ's 2026 LATAM EdTech 100 says about positioning:** AI positioning has moved to
**teacher-facing** tools — instructional support is now more common than consumer tutoring —
and **learning platforms gave up more ground than any other category in the dataset**. The
regional market has already made the choice this KB recommends (sell teacher capability, not
student-facing tutors); arriving with a consumer tutor is arriving a cycle late.

## Regulatory ground truth

This is the part of the file with the longest shelf life, and the part most worth getting right.

| Jurisdiction | What is actually in force | Date |
|--------------|---------------------------|------|

Names **education among six high-risk sectors**; National AI Database pre-registration, conformity assessment, human oversight, **72-hour** incident reporting. 🟢 **`p274`: this is the instrument that RECONCILES the two dates this file called `UNRECONCILED` for five passes** — Law → `Decree 142/2026/ND-CP` (2026-04-30) → `Decision 33/2026/QD-TTg` (education compliance 2027-03-01). **Evidence grade on the pass-266 additions (Vietnam, China anthropomorphic rules, and the
LATAM figures below).** These come from **web-search result text, not from primary sources.**
`WebFetch` against `iesalc.unesco.org` and `digital-strategy.ec.europa.eu` **failed DNS
resolution** through this session's agent proxy, so neither the UNESCO study nor the European
Commission's own AI Act page could be read first-hand this pass. Every figure below is
therefore **secondary-source grade** and marked ⚠️. They are carried because they are the only
numbers available and they are decision-relevant, **not** because they are verified. Dates in
particular conflicted across sources for Vietnam (one source says in force 2026-03-01, another
says compliance due 2027-03-01); the table records the compliance deadline and flags the
conflict rather than picking a winner.

**A later pass with reachable primary sources should re-read:** the UNESCO IESALC study
methodology, Vietnam's July 2026 decision text, and the EU AI Act consolidated dates. The EU
row above is **not** affected — this base resolved the omnibus delay to 2027-12-02 in an
earlier pass, which is more precise than anything this pass's searches returned, and it was
left untouched.

## LATAM adoption, quantified — adoption has outrun governance, and that gap is the engagement

⚠️ **All figures in this section are secondary-source grade** (see the note above).

**UNESCO IESALC**, surveying **200 institutions across 19 countries** in Latin America and the
Caribbean:

| Measure | Figure |
|---------|--------|
| Institutions using AI in **at least one** area | **87%** |
| Using AI in **teaching and learning** | **74%** |
| Institutions with a **formal AI strategy** | **26%** |
| Private non-profit universities implementing | **84%** |
| Public institutions implementing | **68%** |

**TALIS 2024** — secondary-school teachers who had used AI in the previous year:

| Country | Teachers using AI | | |
|---------|------------------|---|---|
| **Brazil** | **56%** | Colombia | 53% |
| **Chile** | **55%** | Costa Rica | 52% |
| *OECD average* | *36%* | | |

Four LATAM countries sit **16–20 points above the OECD average**. The region is not a
late adopter in this category; it is an early adopter with no governance layer.

**The commercial read, and it is the clearest single opportunity in this file:** **87% adopting
against 26% with a strategy** is a **61-point governance deficit**. The work that gap implies —
AI-use policy, model inventories, human-review design, assessment-integrity controls, staff
training, procurement criteria — is advisory-plus-implementation work that **does not require
displacing an incumbent platform**, which is the usual blocker in public-sector education. It
is also the work that becomes mandatory the moment Brazil's `PL 2338` passes with
education-related selection classified high-risk.

**Institutional entry points named in the sources:** UNESCO IESALC (with UNU-IAS), UNESCO
Santiago and Quito, **ECLAC**, FLACSO Ecuador, Uruguay's **Ceibal**, the Digital Education
Council (2026 LATAM survey, 29 institutions) and the **Latin American AI Index (ILIA)**.
UNESCO launched its **Observatory on AI in Education for Latin America and the Caribbean on
2026-04-14**, in Santiago at ECLAC's 2026 forum — a convening body that did not exist for
earlier passes and is a legitimate route into ministry-level conversations.

## APAC adoption, quantified — and what it does to the Japan gap

Population-level AI use, Microsoft figures reported June 2026 (share of working-age people):

| Market | AI use | Market | AI use |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| **Singapore** | **64.3%** | Vietnam | 27.9% |
| New Zealand | 44.1% | **Japan** | **24.7%** |
| Australia | 40.7% | Malaysia | 22.6% |
| South Korea | 40.6% | Philippines | 20.7% |
| Taiwan | 33.8% | **China** | **17.5%** |

A separate regional CIO survey ranks **Singapore first, then Australia, Korea and Taiwan**,
with Japan, India and China behind — consistent with the table.

**On the Japan gap:** this KB has declared "no school-level Japan data" for several passes and
that declaration **still stands**. What is now available is *population* AI use (24.7%), which
is a different measure and must not be substituted for classroom adoption. The gap is narrower
and better bounded, not closed.

**The counter-intuitive read, worth saying in a pitch:** China sits **last** in this table at
17.5% population AI use while running the region's most prescriptive school AI mandates
(Beijing's compulsory 8 hours/year). Mandated curriculum and population adoption are
independent variables in APAC. Sizing an APAC education opportunity from consumer-adoption
data gets the ranking backwards.

### North America

#### 🔴 `p298` · `2026-10-18` — NOTHING NEW, and the refused figure came back a third time

**The mandated leg added no datum this file does not already hold.** The spread, the **36%** share,
the **~10% of institutions with formal AI guidelines** against **71% of US teachers without AI
training**, the **`$55M` Carnegie Mellon + Gates** commitment into gateway courses, the **IBM /
Microsoft / Google** player set, the **no-FDA-equivalent** framing and the **Colorado / Texas**
piecemeal state requirements are all carried above.

🔴 **The one thing that changed is a refusal hardening into a rule.** The **`USD 169M` "government
commitment to responsible AI in higher education"** — downgraded by `p293` because *the source
reports the figure without naming the government*, refused again by `p297` — **was re-served by this
pass's leg from the same unsourced blog.** 🟢 **RE-CIRCULATION IS NOT CORROBORATION.** Three
appearances via one leg reaching one page is one source. **Do not quote it.** The resolving move is a
leg that **names a government** and reaches an appropriation instrument.

🔵 **Opportunity, unchanged and now cheaper to arm.** The North American engagement is still
*"adoption is not the problem; the absence of a defensible policy and a trained workforce is"* —
and as of `p298` the **evidence artefact** is permissive end to end: `inspect_ai` (MIT) +
`pedagogy-benchmark` (MIT) for documented evals, `agent-governance-toolkit` (MIT) for auditable
policy, and — new this pass — **`Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/edfi-oneroster` (Apache-2.0) to speak OneRoster
1.2 off an existing Ed-Fi ODS without waiting on DMS** (`Gap 309`). See `P60`.

#### 🟡 `p297` · `2026-10-18` — held, with ONE cross-leg corroboration and ONE figure this pass REFUSES to carry

**The spread is still live and still disqualifies a single figure.** This pass's leg returned North
America at **$951 M (2024) → $2.3 B (2029)**, ~**15.9% CAGR**, and a regional share of **36%** in one
source against **37.5%** in another. Global 2026 estimates ranged **$10.6 B–$11.4 B** off 2025 bases
of **$6.4 B**, **$7.52 B** and **$8.3 B**. 🔵 **Quote the band and name the source, or quote nothing**
— `p295` said the same and nothing has narrowed since.

**The governance gap is the number that actually sells work, and it widened again.** Only **~10% of
institutions have formal AI guidelines** while **71% of US teachers lack AI training** — against
student usage the same corpus puts at **92%**. 🔴 That is the shape of every North American
engagement this base has described for ten passes: **adoption is not the problem; the absence of a
defensible policy and a trained workforce is.**

🟢 **Public money, named:** **Carnegie Mellon + the Gates Foundation, $55 M** into AI courseware aimed
at **gateway college courses** — the highest-failure-rate, highest-attrition courses in a US degree,
which is exactly where the retention rows on
[`repos/foundations.md`](../repos/foundations.md) (`datakind/student-success-tool`,
`AnubisLMS/Anubis`'s timestamp insights) apply.

🔴 **One figure is REFUSED, not carried.** A *"$169 M government commitment to responsible AI in
higher education in Q1 2026"* appeared in this leg with **no primary source and no naming of which
government**. It is **not** entered on this file. `p295`'s standing rule: an unattributed public-money
figure in a SOW is a liability, not a datum.

🟢 **Cross-leg corroboration — two independent legs, one programme.** This leg reported that
**OpenAI launched a country-level education programme with eight national partners in Q1 2026**. The
**Central Asia** leg of the same pass independently named **Kazakhstan as a member of that inaugural
cohort**, with a seat count. 🔵 Neither leg knew about the other; they agree. **This is the first time
this base has corroborated a North-America-sourced claim from a non-Western leg**, and it is a cheap
check worth repeating: a global programme should show up in the legs of the countries it names.

🔵 **Regulatory posture unchanged and still the architectural constraint:** education AI operates with
minimal external oversight, decisions sit with **individual schools, districts and universities**, and
state action is piecemeal — **Colorado** and **Texas** named this pass, on top of the
**California AB 1159 / Oklahoma / Maryland** cluster `p296` priced.

#### 🟢 `p296` · `2026-10-18` — the regulation is at STATE level, and it now constrains the architecture, not just the rollout

**What the leg returned.** A 2026 legislative tracker counts **134 AI-in-education bills introduced
across 31 states** this session. Three clusters, and each one prices a different design decision:

| state(s) | instrument | what it forbids or requires | architectural consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **California** | **AB 1159** | bars using **student data to train AI models** | 🔴 kills any design that fine-tunes on client student data. Forces RAG-over-corpus instead of training — and makes `lineage-skill`-style provenance a requirement, not a nicety |
| **Oklahoma, Maryland** | state statute | **human oversight required**; AI barred from **high-stakes decisions about students** | the human checkpoint is a legal interface (`T-p288a`), so an autograding pipeline must keep the grade **advisory**. `P10` is the pattern; `autolab/Autolab` + `dodona-edu/dolos` are now the permissive parts |
| **Georgia, Mississippi** | curriculum requirement | AI content added to **computer science requirements** | demand-side, not restriction: a content and teacher-PD engagement, not a platform one |

**Federal.** The **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026 (H.R. 8747)** cleared a House Education
and Workforce Committee markup on **21 July 2026**, largely along party lines; it would let schools
spend federal funds on AI curriculum and literacy. ⚠️ **A committee markup is not a law** — do not
scope a programme against this money yet. A separate student-drafted *STUDENTS FIRST Act of 2026*
proposes guardrails rather than a ban.

**District level is where pilots actually die.** **New York City** has a **one-year moratorium on
student-facing AI through eighth grade**. 🔵 **The consequence for a studio: in a restricted district
the sellable pilot is teacher-facing, and the shelf now has the permissive rows for exactly that** —
[`anthropics/k12-teacher-skills`](https://github.com/anthropics/k12-teacher-skills) (Apache-2.0,
572★), [`microsoft/Shiksha-Copilot`](https://github.com/microsoft/Shiksha-Copilot) (MIT),
[`datakind/student-success-tool`](https://github.com/datakind/student-success-tool) (MIT, 3 015
commits — advisor-facing, never student-facing).

**Canada and Mexico, stated as the thinner findings they are.** Canada launched an **"AI for All"**
strategy on **4 June 2026** with an AI-literacy push and **no AI statute**, and no date for
legislation was confirmed. Mexico has **bills pending but no regulator to enforce them**. 🔴 **Neither
leg returned a Canadian or Mexican education-specific instrument** — the US state layer is the only
place in this region where the law currently reaches the classroom.

⚠️ **Two figures the leg returned that this file will NOT publish as fact:** a **36%** North American
share of the global AI-in-education market, and **60% of US K-12 teachers using AI in 2024-25 (32%
weekly)**. Both come from aggregator statistics round-ups citing no primary instrument. Quote them as
*"vendor-sourced, unverified"* or not at all.

#### 🟢 `p295` (`2026-10-18`) — the buying signal is institutional, and the governance signal is that there is almost none

| datum | figure | source grade |
|---|---|---|
| 🟢 **OpenAI country-level education programme** | **8 national partners**, Q1 2026 | press-attributed |
| 🟢 **Carnegie Mellon + Gates Foundation** | **USD 55 M** into **AI courseware for gateway college courses** | press-attributed |
| 🔴 **institutions with formal AI guidelines** | **10 %** | secondary roundup |
| 🔴 **US teachers lacking AI training** | **71 %** | secondary roundup |
| 🟡 NA share of the global market | **36 %** | vendor |
| 🟡 NA market size | **USD 951 M (2024) → USD 2 303.2 M (2029)**, CAGR **15.9 %** | vendor |

🔵 **The opportunity is the gap between those rows.** Money is arriving at **courseware for gateway
courses** — the highest-failure, highest-enrolment undergraduate courses — while **90 % of
institutions have no formal AI guideline** and **71 % of teachers have had no training**. 🟢 **A
courseware engagement that ships its own governance artefact (usage policy, assessment-integrity
position, staff training) is selling into a vacuum its competitors are ignoring.**

🔴 **And the regulatory ground is genuinely empty, not merely immature:** no FDA-equivalent exists
for educational technology; adoption is decided school-by-school and district-by-district; **Colorado
and Texas** are introducing piecemeal requirements. 🔵 For a US engagement that means the binding
constraint is **procurement and district policy**, not a federal statute — the opposite of EMEA.

#### 🟡 Pass 291 (`2026-10-18`) — one new sizing datum, and a structural point about who regulates

🟢 **New, and it is the first regional sizing figure this shelf has carried with a stated base

FDA-equivalent for educational technology, and adoption is decided school-by-school,
district-by-district. 🔴 **That is the inverse of EMEA**, where Annex III(3) makes the same
systems high-risk by law. A North American engagement sells assurance to a *buyer*; an EMEA one

#### 🟡 Pass 290 (`2026-10-18`) — the first code row on these shelves from a US STATE EDUCATION AGENCY, and three money datums that are all secondary

#### 🟢 Pass 288 — the US regulatory surface is now STATE-BY-STATE AND DENSE, which makes compliance a product feature rather than a legal review

🔵 **Scale of the 2026 session: 134 AI-in-education bills across 31 states** (tracker count,
[`p288-websearch`](../evidence/p288-websearch.2026-10-18-p288.tsv)). Named instruments:

- **California AB 1159** — bars using **student data to train AI models**.
- **Idaho SB 1227** — privacy requirements for school AI tools.
- **Oklahoma, Maryland** — require **human oversight**, bar AI from **high-stakes decisions
  about students**.
- **Federal: K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026 (H.R. 8747)** — advanced out of House
  Education and Workforce markup `2026-07-21`. 🔴 **Advanced, NOT law.**
- 🔴 **No comprehensive national AI law in force in the US, Canada or Mexico.** Canada's June
  2026 *"AI for All"* strategy is **literacy funding, not statute**; Mexico has bills and **no
  enforcing regulator yet**.

🔴 **District policy is moving the opposite way from vendor roadmaps:** New York City holds a
**one-year moratorium on student-facing AI through grade 8**; Katy ISD **bans generative
chatbots K-6**; Columbia Law School's 2026-27 policy allows AI **as a learning aid but not for
submitted coursework**.

🟢 **The opportunity, placed:** a **per-jurisdiction policy configuration layer** is now the
differentiator in North America — the same tutor shipped into NYC, Katy ISD and a permissive
district needs three different enforcement postures, and the regulated unit is *grade band ×
feature*, not *product*. 🔵 **Teacher adoption is the demand signal**: **60%** of US K-12
teachers used AI in 2024-25 and **32%** weekly (aggregator figures, directional), and
TeachBetter.ai's threshold — teachers adopt what saves **5-10 h/week** — is the bar a
deliverable must clear.

🟡 **The special-education opening is specific and, per this pass, unserved by open source.**
IEP/504 is a federally regulated, deadline-driven, document-heavy workflow, and the three open
repos that address it are **one AGPL-3.0** and **two with no usable grant** (see
[`agents/top.md`](../agents/top.md) pass 288). 🔵 `hdronamraju/sped-agent` is the clearest
read on the real requirement set — California day-counting rules, coordinator approval before
anything is sent, **bilingual EN/es statutory parent notices** — and it is **all rights
reserved**, so it is a specification to learn from and not a dependency.

> **🟢 `p287` (2026-10-18) — two additions and one sharpened warning.** New: a **$169 M**
> government commitment to responsible AI in higher education in **Q1 2026**, and an **OpenAI
> country-level education programme with eight national partners**. 🔴 The second is a
> *procurement* signal, not an adoption one: a vendor contracting at national scale closes a
> market before a studio is invited in. 🔴 The governance gap here is **wider than LATAM's** —
> **86 %** student use against **~10 %** of institutions with formal guidelines and **71 %** of
> US teachers untrained — so the compliance surface an engagement must satisfy is **the
> client's own policy**, there being no federal one to point at (state-level only; Colorado and
> Texas named).

| what moved | detail |

The 🔵 **distinction this shelf already draws stands: `2026-07087` is a GRANTS instrument that weights discretionary competitions and binds no school** — which is why a federal-level query reads as an absence to anyone who expects a mandate |

🔵 **The opportunity is unchanged and better dated.** State duties are **enacted and in the past**
(Ohio's policy deadline was `2026-07-01`), the federal layer moves **money, not classrooms**, and the

an *architecture* requirement for any assessment product sold into either.


**Pass 278 — the per-jurisdiction query returns NAMED STATUTES where the federal query returns a
GRANTS instrument, and the two were being read as one thing.** Asked at federal level, the US
answer looks like policy; asked per jurisdiction, **the binding duties are all at state level** and
the federal instrument turns out to govern **money, not classrooms**.

| level | instrument | what it actually binds |
|---|---|---|
| 🟡 **US federal** | **"Final Priority and Definitions — Secretary's Supplemental Priority on Advancing Artificial Intelligence in Education"**, Federal Register doc **2026-07087**, published **13 Apr 2026** | 🔵 **A GRANTS instrument.** It *weights* AI initiatives in the Department's **discretionary grant competitions**. It does not require anything of a school. **Reading it as a schools mandate is the error this leg corrects** |
| 🟡 US federal | **Dear Colleague Letter, 22 Jul 2025** | Existing **formula and discretionary** funds **may** support AI tools and educator training. Follows the **Mar 2025 Executive Order** on Advancing AI Education for American Youth |
| 🟡 US federal | **Dear Colleague Letter, 20 Aug 2026** | Responsible edtech: clear instructional purpose, evidence-based, transparent to parents, **drop tools that do not improve learning**. ⚠️ **One news summary only — the letter itself was not read** |
| 🔴 **Maryland** | **SB 720 (Chapter 634)**, signed **~26 May 2026** | 🔴 **ENACTED** — state guidance, **local AI policies**, **AI literacy**, and **teacher professional development** |
| 🔴 **Idaho** | **SB 1227** | 🔴 **ENACTED** — **AI literacy standards** and **educator training** |
| 🔴 **Ohio** | district duty | 🔴 **Districts had to ADOPT an AI-use policy by 1 Jul 2026** — a policy duty, not a training mandate. **The deadline has passed**, so every Ohio district is already answerable on this |
| 🔴 **Utah** | enacted bills | **AI literacy for middle-school students** |
| 🟡 **Virginia** | **SB 394** | Directs the state education agency to issue guidance **including teacher training** |
| 🟡 **Oregon** | **ODE, "Generative AI in K-12 Classrooms Guidance" v3**, **Jun 2026** | Published state guidance, non-binding |
| 🔵 **US Congress** | **HR 8747**, *K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026* | **INTRODUCED Apr 2026, NOT enacted.** Would let schools use existing grant funds for AI instruction and teacher training |
| 🟡 **Canada** | 🔴 **no binding provincial rule in ON / AB / BC** | **Education is provincial jurisdiction** and the guidance that exists is from ministries, school boards and teacher federations. **BC:** Ministry framework *"Considerations for using AI tools in schools"* (2023), **seven areas**; districts set their own policies; an advisory committee is planned. **Alberta:** **ASBA** *"Artificial Intelligence Policy Guidance"* (**Sept 2024**) + a three-year provincial partnership with **Amii**. **Ontario:** 🔴 **sources CONFLICT** — one states no Ministry memorandum on AI exists, another reports 2026-27 guidance expecting educators to understand responsible AI use, privacy, cyber safety and ethics; the non-government instruments are an **OASBO/ECNO** guideline (2025) and an **OTF discussion paper** (Jan–Mar 2026) |

🟢 **What this changes for an engagement.** A North American proposal has been scoped against a
federal posture. **It should be scoped against a state.** Maryland and Idaho oblige **teacher
training** — a deliverable a studio can actually staff — and **Ohio's district-policy deadline has
already passed**, which makes policy-and-implementation remediation a live, dated need rather than a
forecast. 🔵 **In Canada the absence is the opportunity and should be sold as such:** with no
provincial rule and the Alberta Teachers' Association itself observing there is *"not a lot of
policy at the jurisdictional level"*, the buyer is the **board or district**, and the first
deliverable is the policy, not the tool.

**Pass 277 — a second institutional-strategy number arrives, it is 92%, and the scope is the whole
finding.** EDUCAUSE's **"The Impact of AI on Work in Higher Education"** (fielded **29 Sep – 13 Oct
2025**, **n = 1,960** responses meeting inclusion criteria) reports that **92% of respondents'
institutions have a work-related AI strategy**, that **69%** of those with workforce-skills goals are
pursuing them primarily by **upskilling and reskilling existing faculty and staff**, and that **81%**
report enthusiasm or mixed caution-and-enthusiasm.

🔴 **This shelf already carries "only about 1 in 3 universities has a clear AI strategy" (Ellucian).
92% and ~33% are not a contradiction, and treating them as one would be the error.** The instruments
measure different objects: EDUCAUSE asked about a **work-related** AI strategy — AI in the *employment
and operations* of the institution — while the Ellucian figure is an **institution-wide** AI strategy
covering teaching and learning. 🔵 **This is the third consecutive pass in which a North American
figure turned out to be scoped rather than wrong** (`p276` refused a floating *"~10% have formal AI
guidelines"* for having no scope attached; `p275`'s LATAM correction separated a *perception* measure
from a *policy count*). **The rule this shelf keeps re-deriving: an adoption percentage without its
object is not a number, and the object is usually the pitch.**

🟢 **How to use both, without splicing them.** Quote **92%** when the engagement is **operational** —
staff workflow, administration, workforce enablement; the buyer already believes they have a strategy
there, so the sale is execution. Quote **~1 in 3** when the engagement is **academic** — teaching,
learning, assessment; that is where the strategy is genuinely missing. **Never add them, average them,
or present either as "institutions with an AI strategy" unqualified.**

🟢 **One forward-looking instrument:** **AI tops the 2027 EDUCAUSE Top 10 IT Issues list for the first
time in the survey's history, displacing cybersecurity.** For a North American higher-ed engagement
that is the budget signal — the member-surveyed priority list is what IT leadership plans against.

⚠️ **GRADE: SECONDARY.** `educause.edu` was not read first-hand (DNS-unresolvable here). The **92%**
figure is also from a survey **fielded in autumn 2025**, so it is a year old against this pass's date —
quote it with its field window, not as "current".

**Pass 270 — three North America datums discharged from the backlog `p269` registered, and two of
the three were WRONG as the register carried them.**

- 🟢 **US DOL AI Literacy Framework — confirmed, and it now has a citable identifier.** Issued
  **2026-02-13** as **Training and Employment Notice No. 07-25** (one secondary source renders it
  *TEGL* 07-25; the rendering is **unreconciled** and both are recorded). **Five content areas** —
  understanding AI principles, exploring potential uses, directing AI effectively, evaluating AI
  outputs, using AI responsibly — and **seven delivery principles**, including experiential
  learning, embedding learning in context, and building complementary human skills. It is
  **voluntary guidance and imposes no new regulatory requirement**; its named audiences are
  **workforce boards, community colleges and Job Corps centres**, and it sits on top of earlier DOL
  guidance encouraging **WIOA funds and governor's reserve monies** to be spent on AI skilling.
  🔵 **For the studio this is a funding instrument, not a compliance one** — the five content areas
  are a ready-made curriculum spec for a publicly-funded AI-literacy deliverable, and the delivery
  principles are the acceptance criteria. *(secondary-source grade.)*

- 🔴 **`STUDENTS FIRST Act of 2026` is NOT legislation, and this base has been shelving it next to
  bills.** It is a **student-written model policy**: **98 teenagers from all 50 states**, a mock
  Senate session in Boston, **passed 82-16 on 2026-07-19**. It has **no legal force at any level**
  and no district is obliged by it. Its route to influence is **AASA circulating the text to its
  ~10,000 superintendent members** (publication **2026-08-03** — which is the "August 2026" this
  base already carried). Contents: AI banned on all graded tests; AI may not do the writing, but
  may be used for editing, brainstorming and studying **after 8th grade**; states must provide
  AI-literacy instruction once students are given classroom devices; GenAI vendors must disclose
  known biases to educational customers. The students **rejected** an absolute parental veto.
  🔴 **The hazard was adjacency, not ignorance:** `repos/trending.md` lists it inside the same cell
  as `H.R. 8747`, `AB 1159` and `SB 1227` — three real instruments — with no status marker. A
  reader building a compliance slide from that cell would brief a student exercise as pending law.
  🟢 **Shelved here with the marker: `NOT-LAW · model policy · advisory only`.** *(The acronym's
  expansion is inconsistent across sources and is deliberately NOT resolved here.)*

- 🔴 **North Carolina has THREE layers, and the backlog datum was the oldest and weakest of them.**
  The register's "North Carolina's K-12 implementation guidance" is **NC DPI's generative-AI
  recommendations for PK-13 public schools, released 2024-01-16** (with AI for Education) — a
  2024 artefact, not a 2026 one. The **2026** layer is the **NC AI Leadership Council AI Strategic
  Roadmap, released 2026-07-01**, which DPI **"strongly recommends"** and which tells districts to
  vet incumbent EdTech GenAI vendors for **safety, privacy, reliability and efficacy** — without
  specifying how, so practice will vary by district. The third layer is already on this base as
  **`P115-S`: Session Law 2026-41 §7.39**, effective **2026-07-01**. 🟢 **Unlike Vietnam (`p269`),
  these three do not contradict each other — they LAYER**, oldest advisory to newest statute, and
  the statute and the Roadmap share a commencement date. 🔴 **But shelving the backlog datum alone
  would have published NC's 2024 position as its current one.**
  ⚠️ **Adoption counter-evidence, and it is the engagement-shaping fact:** a **WRAL report of
  February 2026** found only **~one third of NC district leaders had given students any GenAI
  training**, with many teachers not using it and many prohibiting it for assignments. **North
  Carolina is a vet-the-incumbent-vendors and train-the-teachers engagement, not a greenfield
  build** — the same adoption-risk-over-penalty-risk shape `T22` established for Korea.

**Pass 266 addition — the assessment-integrity opening is now permissively tooled.** Oklahoma's
`SB 1734` (educator supervision, human review, AI barred from high-stakes decisions, annual
parent disclosure), Ohio's `HB 96` (board-approved AI policy in every district) and California's
`AB 1159` (no student data for model training) all describe the *same* technical requirement:
**an assessment system whose AI is auditable, overridable and switchable off.**
[`ls1intum/Artemis`](https://github.com/ls1intum/Artemis) (MIT) is that shape by construction —
its Iris/Athena/Hyperion subsystems are **config-gated**, so "AI off" is a deployment setting
rather than a rebuild, and `AB 1159` compliance is a configuration claim a district can verify.
Pair with [`jupyter/nbgrader`](https://github.com/jupyter/nbgrader) (BSD-3-Clause) for the
notebook-based courses that dominate US data-science teaching. **The pitchable sentence:** a
district can adopt AI assistance and demonstrate, by configuration, that no high-stakes decision
was made by a model.

The largest market (36% share) and by far the most fragmented regulatory surface:
134 bills across 31 states means a national product does not exist — **compliance
configurability is the product**.

- **Per-jurisdiction policy enforcement as a feature.** `AB 1159`-style data-training bans,
  Oklahoma/Maryland human-oversight mandates and NYC's grade-banded moratorium are
  contradictory by design. Moodle 5.1's per-course/per-activity AI toggles map almost exactly
  onto this requirement; wire district policy to those toggles and the audit trail writes itself.
- **Human-in-the-loop assessment.** Where AI is barred from high-stakes decisions, the
  sellable architecture is `Artemis` (MIT, automated *feedback*) plus explicit teacher sign-off
  — not autonomous grading. `Claw-ED`'s teacher-reviewed posture is the right default.
- **Incumbent migration.** Canvas and Blackboard renewals are the natural trigger for an
  Open edX or Moodle migration with a client-owned AI layer.
- **AI literacy curriculum delivery.** `H.R. 8747` would let schools spend federal funds on AI
  curriculum. If it passes, procurement follows, and `instructional_agents` (MIT) is the
  content-generation engine for exactly that.
- **Policy exists, governance does not — and that is the retainer.** Roughly **34 states plus
  DC and Puerto Rico** have issued AI guidance, while **86% of education organisations use
  generative AI and most have no policy**. Reported student use is **92% of university
  students and 54% of K-12 students**. The gap between use and governance is not a product
  gap; it is an advisory-plus-implementation engagement, and it has a deadline attached in
  every state that now *mandates* a district policy (**Ohio `HB 96`**, **Idaho `SB 1227`**).
- **"AI may not replace a teacher" is an architectural requirement, not a slogan.** Idaho's
  enacted `SB 1227` says it outright, Oklahoma's `SB 1734` requires educator supervision plus
  **annual parent disclosure**. Build the disclosure artefact and the human-sign-off record as
  first-class features — `Claw-ED`'s teacher-reviewed posture plus an `lrsql`/xAPI audit trail
  — and one architecture satisfies Idaho, Oklahoma and NYC at once.
- **Higher education is the under-served half.** Arizona's `HB 4040` would push student-AI-use
  policies into public universities and New Jersey's bill would require colleges to *offer* AI
  certificate and degree programmes. Both are bills, not law; both point at university
  procurement with nobody incumbent. `Artemis` (MIT) and `Autolab` (Apache-2.0) are the
  permissive university-grade bases, and `nbgrader` (BSD-3) is already inside the data-science
  departments that will ask first.

**Pass 267 — the North America section had the market structurally wrong, and the fix enlarges it.**

- 🔴 **Canvas is open source, and this KB said otherwise.** [`instructure/canvas-lms`](https://github.com/instructure/canvas-lms)
  is **AGPL-3.0 with 78,765 commits** — the largest education codebase this KB has located. The
  previous framing (Canvas as closed SaaS, *"the incumbent to migrate off"*) would have produced a
  needlessly adversarial pitch into the dominant platform of North American higher education. **The
  opening is the opposite of migration:** deploy or extend Canvas, reach it through
  [`vishalsachdev/canvas-mcp`](https://github.com/vishalsachdev/canvas-mcp) (**MIT**, 295★, 102–126
  tools), and keep the deliverable outside the AGPL boundary. **AGPL platform, MIT doorway** — no
  platform decision asked of the client, which is the fastest engagement shape in this file.
- 🟢 **The permissive assessment stack here is deeper than the regulatory requirement.** NA-origin
  and all permissive: [`Submitty`](https://github.com/Submitty/Submitty) (BSD-3, RPI, 9,316
  commits, **1.1k forks over 802 stars**), [`otter-grader`](https://github.com/ucbds-infra/otter-grader)
  (BSD-3, UC Berkeley, **Python and R**, ships a **Canvas adapter**),
  [`Autolab`](https://github.com/autolab/Autolab) (Apache-2.0, CMU), `nbgrader` (BSD-3), on
  [`jupyterhub`](https://github.com/jupyterhub/jupyterhub) (BSD-3, **8.4k★**). Where state law bars
  AI from determining a grade, **this stack grades deterministically and the AI explains, suggests
  and tutors around it** — the P10 posture, now with five permissive components instead of three.
- 🟡 **Do not brief TRAIGA or the RAISE Act as education regulation.** Texas **TRAIGA (HB 149)** was
  signed 2025-06-22 and took effect **2026-01-01** — AG-exclusive enforcement, a **60-day cure
  period**, no private right of action, and an **affirmative defence for substantial compliance with
  the NIST AI Risk Management Framework** (the one genuinely actionable item: align the deliverable
  to NIST AI RMF and document it). New York's **RAISE Act** targets **frontier-model developers
  spending >$100M on compute** — it does **not** reach an education deployment, and its effective
  date is **disputed** across sources (2026-03-19 vs. an amended January 2027). ⚠️ Secondary-source;
  the education-specific obligations in this section's state laws above remain the ones that bite.
- 🔵 **Remaining hole, stated:** no North American *public-sector education platform* was located.
  The public-sector code found this pass is a **service** layer (the UK's `DFE-Digital`, MIT), not a
  platform. For US K-12 public work the installed base is still Canvas, Moodle and PowerSchool-class
  commercial SIS — integration, not adoption.
- 🔴 **CORRECTED IN PASS 268 — the hole above is wrong, and the correct statement is a better
  commercial position than the one it replaces.** North America publishes a permissive
  public-sector education layer: the **Ed-Fi** stack —
  [`Ed-Fi-Data-Standard`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/Ed-Fi-Data-Standard),
  [`Ed-Fi-ODS`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/Ed-Fi-ODS),
  [`Data-Management-Service`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/Data-Management-Service),
  [`Ed-Fi-ODS-Implementation`](https://github.com/Ed-Fi-Alliance-OSS/Ed-Fi-ODS-Implementation) —
  **all Apache-2.0, all payload-verified 2026-10-17.** It is not a platform; it is the **data
  standard and system of record** that US districts and state agencies already model students in.
  🟢 **Why that is the stronger pitch:** in a market where the LMS is already bought, owning the
  *interoperability* layer reaches every vendor and every district at once, and the Apache grant
  means the deliverable carries no copyleft boundary — unlike Canvas (AGPL-3.0) or Open edX
  (AGPL-3.0). The engagement shape is *conformance and agent surface*, not platform replacement.
  ⚠️ **Do not brief a state adoption count** — see the pass-268 gap note below.

*National Academy for AI Instruction*, a partnership with Microsoft, OpenAI and Anthropic.** 🔵 Why it
matters commercially: it is a **teacher-training** institution built by the **union**, not by a district
or a vendor. For a North American engagement that means the professional-development lane has an
incumbent with labour-side legitimacy — partner with it or route around it deliberately, but do not
propose teacher upskilling into this market as though the space were empty.

CAGR produces `$10.8B`. If a North America deck in circulation quotes `$32B`, it came from this base and
it needs correcting. See the pass-271 section above.

🔵 **Everything else this pass's NA leg returned was already held** — Canada's *AI for All* (2026-06-04),
**134 bills across 31 states** in 2026, `H.R. 8747`, `AB 1159`, NYC's moratorium, North Carolina's three
layers, the >200-company federal pledge, Georgia and Mississippi's CS credits. 🟢 **Recorded as a
measured nil on the NA regulatory channel**, not as an absence of searching.

#### Pass 275 addendum — the US governance picture moved, and it moved toward the states

🟡 **Thin leg, one usable datum.** A **December executive order** directs challenges to *conflicting
state* AI laws, and the same reporting holds that **states remain the primary driver of US AI
governance**. 🔵 For an education engagement this **sharpens** the higher-education procurement lane
`p274` closed rather than contradicting it: the instrument a US institution must satisfy is still
**FERPA plus its own state's AI statute**, and the federal move is an attempt to narrow state
divergence, not to replace it with a federal rule. **Plan for state-by-state variation and do not wait
for a federal standard to arrive.**

⚠️ **One concrete institutional datum, single-sourced and flagged:** **Boston Public Schools** is
reported as the first major US district to make **AI fluency a graduation requirement**, from
**September 2026**. Not corroborated; do not put it in a client deck without a second source.

🔴 **Two market figures were returned and NEITHER is published.** One source gives North America
`$951M (2024) → $2.3B (2029)`; another gives a `41.7%` North America growth rate. Those are
**inconsistent by roughly an order of magnitude** on the same region, and `p272` established that this
region's dollar figures trace back to a single upstream decomposition anyway. Nothing was added to the
headline table. Query: `AI education North America 2026 adoption regulation players`.

#### Pass 289 (`2026-10-18`) — North America

🟢 **The richest regional leg of the pass, and the binding layer is sub-federal.**
**134 AI-in-education bills across 31 states in 2026.** Named instruments:

- **California AB 1159** — restricts training AI models on student data
- **Idaho SB 1227** — requires privacy protections for school AI tools
- **Oklahoma** and **Maryland** — require human oversight and **bar AI from high-stakes
  decisions about students**
- **Georgia** and **Mississippi** — add AI content to computer-science credits
- Federal: **K-12 AI Literacy and Readiness Act of 2026 (H.R. 8747)** advanced in a July markup
- Districts: **NYC** one-year moratorium on student-facing AI **through grade 8**;
  **Katy ISD (TX)** bans GenAI chatbots K-6 with supervised phase-in to grade 12
- 🇨🇦 **Canada**: *"AI for All"* national strategy launched **2026-06-04**, AI-literacy push,
  **no legislative date**. 🇲🇽 **Mexico**: bills, **no regulator yet**

**Adoption:** **60%** of US K-12 teachers used AI tools in 2024-25, **32%** at least weekly.

🔵 **The opportunity, placed:** a 50-state patchwork plus district moratoria is a
**policy-configuration** problem before it is a modelling problem. The sellable artefact is an
agent whose grading and recommendation paths can be **turned off per jurisdiction and
evidenced** — Oklahoma/Maryland's human-oversight rule and NYC's K-8 moratorium are the two
hardest configurations, and they are specifiable today.

builds on state-published weights; in Kazakhstan the studio's opening is the **governance, evaluation
and integration layer around a platform the state has already bought** — which is precisely the layer

#### 🔴 `p296` · `2026-10-18` — THE EU HIGH-RISK DEADLINE FOR EDUCATION MOVED BY SIXTEEN MONTHS, AND THE DUTY THAT BINDS TODAY DID NOT MOVE AT ALL

🔴 **This is the most consequential single finding of the pass, and it inverts the urgency this base
has been briefing.** The **Digital Omnibus on AI** moved the **Annex III stand-alone high-risk
obligations — which is where education sits** — from **2 August 2026 to 2 December 2027.** Embedded
high-risk systems under Annex I moved to **2 August 2028**.

| obligation | applies | status |
|---|---|---|
| **AI literacy duty (Art. 4)** | **since 2 February 2025** | 🔴 **NOT delayed. In force now, for every organisation that uses an AI system — therefore every school** |
| **Prohibited practices** | in force | 🔴 **NOT delayed** |
| **High-risk duties for education** (admissions ranking, student evaluation, exam scoring) | **2 December 2027** | 🟢 moved back from 2 Aug 2026 |
| High-risk embedded in regulated products (Annex I) | 2 August 2028 | moved back |

🔵 **What this does to an EMEA engagement, which is the only thing that matters here.** The
compliance-deadline pitch — *"your assessment system must be conformant by August 2026"* — **is no
longer true, and a client who has been told it will discover that.** What is true, and is the better
engagement anyway: **the Art. 4 AI-literacy duty already binds, and the high-risk classification is
unchanged** — admissions ranking, student evaluation and exam scoring are still Annex III. So the
sixteen extra months are **build time, not relief**: scope the literacy obligation now (it is due),
and design the assessment system to the high-risk standard now, because the classification did not
change and retrofitting conformance into a shipped grading pipeline is the expensive path. `P2`
should be re-dated to the **December 2027** window.

⚠️ **The dates are contested and must be cited carefully.** One source has the Council giving final
approval on **29 June 2026** with Official Journal publication pending; the Commission's own page says
it **entered into force 27 July 2026**. The Commission is the more authoritative of the two, but
**check the Official Journal before putting a date in a SOW** — this base is not able to resolve it
from secondary sources.

**Supply side and institutions.** The **European Commission and the OECD** published *Empowering
Learners for the Age of AI: An AI Literacy Framework for Primary and Secondary Education* on **18 June
2026** — directly usable as the curriculum spine for an Art. 4 engagement. The **European AI Office**
runs implementation, especially for general-purpose AI.

🟢 **The region's permissive supply improved this pass, from the orphan list rather than from a
search:** [`dodona-edu/dolos`](https://github.com/dodona-edu/dolos) — **MIT, 352★, 3 838 commits,
Ghent University** — is academic-integrity evidence, and it is the layer that makes a *student
evaluation* system defensible under an Annex III classification.

⚠️ **The depth of the regional gap, stated plainly.** A Council of Europe survey found **only 4 of 23
responding member states** had education AI policies and regulations. That survey is **2022** and is
the most recent this leg returned — 🔴 **so the per-member-state picture is four years stale and this
file will not pretend otherwise.** An informed gap: no current pan-European inventory of national
education-AI policy was findable this pass.
